But it's the same with this proposal that a solution has been made for this. The Enhedslisten has endorsed a report from SF's motion for a resolution B 17, which means that the government is preparing a comprehensive analysis based on the many topics mentioned in the motion for a resolution, and these are very many important topics for the Enhedslisten. The catalogue must be presented to the Rural and Islands Committee by 15 September and it must enter into a debate on an agreement on a plan of action so that this can be done in time for the financial law negotiations. We have never really been that far with concrete decisions on rural conditions. Of the many topics to be included in the catalogue, specific mention is made of state jobs. So that means that a decision has been taken that we have achieved that by 15 September there must be a catalogue that can be negotiated and included in the financial law negotiations. The motion for a resolution tabled by the Danish People's Party will then lead to a duplication of work in the Danish Parliament, and with the reputation and reputation of the parliamentary work in the public, I do not think that is a good thing. Instead of the Danish People's Party, we call on the Danish People's Party to enter into the concrete negotiations that S and SF have secured through the decision in B 17 on a plan of action for the strengthening of rural areas, and this is therefore a proposal which is at best superfluous.

There has been a clear tendency for supranational convention legislation to decide, and it is basing and binding national legislation, so that national legislation disappears, as it were, out of parliament, where it is under discussion, and into some supranational conventions. This trend has also been called global governance, that is, supranational legislation, which is increasingly replacing national legislation. A fundamental reflection on what we are discussing today must therefore be based on the constitution, the rule of the people, the parliament and the national right of self-determination on the one hand and the law of the convention on the other. As you know, the Stateless Convention dates from 1961, and the first discussions on statelessness began immediately after World War II. You might think that it's time for a service check. Is it really appropriate, or is it not rather appalling, that potential terrorists, for example, should be given citizenship because the convention is placed on account of Denmark and the Danes? ParlaMint-DK_20150521110130	Thank you for the floor. The 1961 UN Convention on the Reduction of Statelessness, the so-called stateless Convention, which we are discussing today, was ratified by Denmark in 1977. This happened when, with the necessary majority in this Chamber, the changes to the law of citizenship were made. The aim of the Convention is to limit statelessness as far as possible, and this is an important objective, as possession of citizenship can be absolutely fundamental to participation in society and a prerequisite for access to a number of key rights. Although it is now more than 50 years since the stateless Convention was adopted, unfortunately the Convention remains relevant. The government has taken the fundamental decision that we want to live up to our international commitments, and with this I have seen the answer to the question for today's discussion. I would also like to point out that, in my view, it is deeply problematic that we are forced to grant citizens Danish citizenship and Danish citizenship when we know that they may be a threat to the security of the state, and this is a dilemma and a problem which the Folketing is fully aware of, for it has been going on for a number of years. On this occasion I can inform the Danish Parliament that, fortunately, there are very few people who gain access to Danish citizenship through the stateless Convention. In 2014 it was one person, and in 2013 fortunately only three people. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20150521110418	There is no doubt that we in the Danish People's Party would have liked to have done away with the fundamentalism of the Convention, which has influenced Danish and European legislation, and the fact that supranational convention legislation increasingly determines Danish legislation and thereby undermines national and popular self-determination. One is the conventions themselves, and another is it here with interpreters and experts who must constantly lecture us about whether one or the other political decision in the area of refugees, for example, is a breach of the Convention or, as it also states, is contrary to international obligations. I would like to mention, as an example, Jonas Christoffersen of the Institute for Human Rights and his and the Centre's for me to see quite a violent response to the government's tightening up of family reunification rules. This was done in a post in Jyllands-Posten on 5 December last year. It is very difficult in the speech to see what is ideology and a political attempt to influence legislation and what is an interpretation or a sober assessment of what the conventions actually say. The conventions have gone from pious declarations of intent, which could be drawn out of the drawer on special occasions, to an extremely powerful political instrument which is used to produce supranational legislation which, for us to see, in fact short-circuits the democratic decision-making process. And I would also say that this is contrary to the spirit of the Constitution. A good example is the stateless convention. It means that people born and raised in the country automatically acquire citizenship because they are stateless and therefore do not have to fulfil conditions that apply to everyone else. This has meant that consideration for Denmark and the safety of the Danes has been lower than consideration for the Convention. We have had a good time, so I can now understand the Minister of Justice, and we have actually asked questions about it in a total of four cases since 2011, when PET has warned against a person who was a threat to the security of the realm but who, because this person was a stateless person, has nevertheless been granted citizenship. Whatever the point, it is grotesque for potential terrorists to acquire citizenship as a result of a convention. But there is a majority in the Danish Parliament that has applauded that. It also shows that the consideration of supranational convention legislation has weighed more heavily than the consideration of Denmark. Since they were drafted and adopted, the conventions have been surrounded by a certain form of holiness which has made them untouchable regardless of common sense. In any case, we have been told that the straitjacket of the conventions should be taken on board. There is nothing untouchable or sacred about the conventions for us. They are international contractual documents which, of course, can be changed when they are obsolete. I had hoped that the Minister would at least admit it, but we did not really get anything out of it either. The very fact that in Denmark citizenship is granted to a person whom PET considers to be a threat to the security of the empire and which can therefore be a potential terrorist, and the fact that this is happening on the basis of a convention alone, is enough for us to say stop and call for us to start seriously looking at whether the stateless convention should be changed or whether we should not rather step out of it. Basically, we believe that it is contrary to the spirit of the constitution that a convention should be allowed to dictate what we in Denmark should think when it is done against the interests of Denmark and the security of the Danes. According to Section 44, the Constitution ensures that it is the Folketing and the Folketing alone that grants citizenship by law. For us, the conventions have never been anything but declarations of intent in relation to the constitution, and therefore the conventions cannot be used as an absolute argument for example. to grant citizenship to potential terrorists. Of course, the Constitution will always trump a convention, as the Constitution is the foundation of all other legislation. That is why it is crucial for us that we now take lifeholds with, among others. The stateless Convention. This is for the sake of the constitution and the rule of the people. We sometimes think that this fight has been lonely, but we welcome the fact that there are political porridges and something is happening. As I said, I had hoped that the Minister would have been more accommodating on this issue, but that does not seem to be the case. But I can certainly see that the Left has indicated in several rounds that it would like to see whether the stateless Convention should be changed. In an interview to Altinget on 11 May, Mr. Karsten Lauritzen from the Left that he will follow David Cameron and make up for the straitjacket of the international conventions, as he puts it. We welcome this in the Danish People's Party. I hope, then, that the rapporteur of the Left will confirm today that this also applies to the stateless Convention. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20150521111900	Thank you to the rapporteur. Mrs Schmidt-Nielsen as rapporteur for the Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521111905	Stateless people live without a large number of the civil rights that we others take for granted. They do not have the right to vote, they do not have the freedom to travel freely like the rest of us. In a wide range of areas, their rights are limited. Only in very rare cases is statelessness self-inflicted. The vast majority of stateless persons are born without citizenship. Unfortunately, the 1961 UN Convention on the Reduction of Statelessness is still in force. One could say that it is sadly relevant because there are still a great many people, horribly many people in the world who are born without citizenship. In the Enhedslisten, we want both Denmark and all other countries to do everything we possibly can to limit this phenomenon. After all, we believe that the aim must be for all people to be born with citizenship and that no one will end up in a situation where they belong nowhere. That is why, of course, we also support the stateless Convention. After all, it allows stateless persons who are born and raised in Denmark to obtain Danish citizenship under conditions that are more relaxed than those of others. We think it is sensible to grant special rights to people who have been born stateless, and I also think it is sensible for Denmark that we do what we can to ensure that the people who have been born in Denmark and have lived their entire lives here become Danish citizens, become part of the Danish community. So we support the Convention and do not believe that Denmark should withdraw.

ParlaMint-DK_20150521114647	I think the title of this motion for a resolution is misleading, because the title reads, and I quote: 'Proposal for a parliamentary resolution that foreigners, who are being warned against by PET, should not be granted citizenship and should not be subject to legislation on the notification of the right of entry'. According to Article 44 of the Constitution, citizenship is granted by law. The legislators in this country are members of the Folketing, and it must be said that the legislators should not legislate blindly. These cases are very rare. The authors themselves write in their comments on the motion for a resolution that since June 2011 there have been three cases in which stateless persons who were suspected by PET and were covered by the Convention have acquired citizenship. The then Minister for Integration, Mr. After all, Søren Pind tried to launch a debate in June 2011 on how Denmark could avoid giving citizenship to this type of applicant, and the then government approached the UN High Commissioner for Refugees, UNHCR, and got the clear answer that, according to the contractual obligations which Denmark has agreed to, a stateless person born and raised in Denmark must have been found guilty of a crime against the security of the State or have been sentenced to a prison sentence of at least 5 years before he or she could be denied citizenship. In this way, I believe, the stateless Convention sets an appropriate standard of legal certainty which, in the view of the Enhedslisten, should apply to everyone, not just those who are so unfortunate to be born stateless. I think it is problematic that, because they have someone under suspicion, PET can say: this person should not be given citizenship. I think it must be the case that if PET finds it problematic that a person gets a Danish passport, Special Branch must ensure that they are prosecuted in court so that the court can decide whether or not this person is guilty. A suspicion on a secret basis should not be enough to cut people off from citizenship. So we cannot support the resolution. We are, in fact, satisfied with the legal certainty guaranteed by the Convention.

ParlaMint-DK_20150521115359	The Danish People's Party is making an interesting suggestion here that foreigners suspected of endangering the security of the empire should not be granted citizenship and that PET's suspicions should outweigh the concerns of the UN Convention on Statelessness. The practice today is, of course, that people are removed from a bill on citizenship if PET warns in advance that a given applicant is a threat to the security of the empire. However, in relation to the stateless persons' convention, there are more lenient rules for stateless persons who are refused only if they have been sentenced to more than five years in prison or have been specifically convicted of crimes against the security of the State. Basically, in the Conservative People's Party, we believe that there is a very good reason why Denmark is actively seeking to influence the UN to amend the Convention on Statelessness, even to withdraw from it, because we think it is unreasonable that the requirements are so much more relaxed for stateless persons. But we are also in line with other rapporteurs concerned about this motion for a resolution. It is laid down in the Constitution. After all, in the previous debate we have just had a discussion that the Constitution is the supreme law we have, and it is therefore written down that no foreigner can have citizenship without law. It is therefore a political decision which will be given citizenship in Denmark. That is why we in the Conservative People's Party are also concerned that this proposal might be at odds with the Constitution. Because the proposal means that, if PET suspects that an applicant is a threat to the security of the country, it can cause the processing of the application to be interrupted and the application to be totally rejected, thus becoming an administrative assessment and not a political assessment. And we therefore believe that the political assessment is important. So we reject the proposal because we are concerned about whether it is unconstitutional and because we want a political and not an administrative position. Finally, however, I would just like to stress that we are very much in agreement with the Danish People's Party that if you are planning to commit terrorism in Denmark or otherwise endanger the security of the empire, you should not, of course, have citizenship.

Specifically, the Norwegian model is being asked in the motion for a resolution, and the proposers point out that both Norway, Iceland and Switzerland have concluded parallel agreements. In fact, this is not quite the case; on the contrary, it is what is called operational agreements concluded by Europol with a number of third countries. The operational agreements cannot be equated with full membership of Europol. For example, there is no direct access to the systems that apply to Europol, and these are the ones we would like to have access to for the Danish police. ParlaMint-DK_20150521130426	Thank you very much, President. It is good for Denmark because an opt-in system is a much better tool in the fight against crime than a parallel agreement can ever be. In the Left, we want to combat the cross-border crime that exists in Denmark and we want those responsible to be able to be prosecuted and punished very much. In the words of the National Police, we can only do so if we are in Europol. The Danish police are also a diligent user of Europol. In 2013, the Danish police used Europol in 315 cases in concrete and cross-border investigations, and that same year the Danish police received more than 3,000 requests through Europol. But new tools are constantly needed for the police. Cross-border crime is now two steps ahead, which is why the Danish opt-in model also contains elements other than just membership of Europol. It is imperative that Denmark also participate in the other parts of EU cooperation on combating the sexual abuse of children, the collection of evidence in cross-border criminal proceedings and European police restraining orders. ParlaMint-DK_20150521141413	The debate is open, and that is Mr. Karsten Lauritzen as rapporteur for the Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521141448	The bill we are dealing with here is a bill which gives PET, i.e. the Police Intelligence Service, access via SKAT to the information that airlines record about passengers and crew members in connection with travel to and from Denmark. Paragraph 148a of the Aviation Act was introduced with anti-terrorism package II in 2006, when we had a bourgeois government and that allowed PET to obtain PNR data. However, it has been found not to be possible to establish direct access for PET and therefore, through cooperation with SKAT, PET has had access to PNR data under the Customs Act. However, the cooperation between PET and SKAT has been suspended because there have been doubts about the legal basis, but it is a little difficult for me, when I read the proposal, to see immediately when exactly that has happened, so that is something we need to look at in committee. It is, of course, scandalous in itself if one has had a practice that has not been provided for. So we need to look into that. That is why PET has, and that is what is currently the result, at least at present, when it is not cases covered by the Code of Procedure, i.e. with a court order, not the possibility of obtaining PNR data from the airlines, and this proposal must then be corrected. The bill amends the Customs Act so that airlines are obliged to provide SKAT with information on passengers and crews of aircraft, i.e. aircraft they hold. Furthermore, the draft law amends the PET Act so that SKAT must pass on information about passengers and crews of aircraft to PET if the service considers that the information can be provided With the proposal, it is therefore proposed that SKAT be given a separate legal basis for receiving and transmitting information to PET. In view of the fact that the level of threat to Denmark has changed, it is clear, of course, that we need a strong defence against terrorism. We are in an unfortunately extraordinary situation today in which there is war and conflict in Syria and Iraq, and we have to recognise that there are some of these foreign fighters who are Danish nationals or people with permanent residence in Denmark who travel to Denmark, among others. Syria, which, unfortunately, when they return, poses a security risk. The Left wants us to have a good and safe guard against terrorism. On the other hand, however, citizens should never experience unnecessary suspicion and get into a system without having done anything other than travel by plane, and it is therefore always essential that we consider whether there is proportionality in the things we propose when we discuss actions that balance on the edge of privacy. Unfortunately, I cannot at this stage give an undertaking that the Left will be able to support this proposal. I hope that I will be able to do so in the course of the committee procedure which is due. There are therefore some things we need to look at in committee. And this is such a first step towards the PNR register that the European Union is working on and that we in the Left have said we are positive about. However, we have also mentioned a few concerns. In this bill, which is a first step, we wonder, among other things. that you choose to exchange this information with PET via SKAT. The model you can discuss maybe you should give PET a direct legal basis instead. We also wonder a little bit about not including those legal guarantees, for example. in relation to access to the information that is being stored about one and that currently lies in the EU action, which will be adopted this year in 2015 • that is to say this year; it is the plan and then, once we have removed the legal reservation, it will probably and hopefully have to be implemented in Denmark. So it's a step in the right direction. We acknowledge that, but then we also think we should take the right direction when it comes to legal certainty, and not just in terms of being an effective tool for the intelligence service. However, I have to acknowledge that the proposal makes reference to the fact that the notices to be made on exactly what information is to be collected and how it is to be implemented in the form of notices are to be discussed with the PET inspectorate. We appreciate that. However, and I shall do so in the end, I have to make a criticism. Because this is an important proposal, but I must criticise the fact that it has been put forward so late. In other words, we have some rules in the Folketing that the government's bill should be tabled before 1 April, and sometimes a few days may pass over it, at least under the bourgeois government, but I think that the times when it happened we also received criticism from the parties that were in opposition. It was a necessary criticism, and I therefore think it should also be mentioned here that it is unsatisfactory that this bill is being tabled so late in the assembly and I am concerned that it is possible to have a proper and thorough legal procedure. I am also a little surprised that this proposal presented by the government in their terrorist campaign in February is only being discussed today in the Folketing. Unfortunately, that is not satisfactory. But we must look at this during the committee procedure and then the Left will take up its position there. But I certainly hope that we can find ourselves in a situation where we can support the proposal, because it contains a large number of necessary things in the fight against terrorism.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521142003	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mrs Trine Bramsen from the Socialists. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521142012	In Denmark one must feel safe, and that is why we Social Democrats are very concerned that our police have the resources and tools to prevent terrorists from coming to Denmark and succeeding in their totally unsavory attacks. Because terrorists constantly develop new methods and move across borders, we must, of course, ensure that our police have the necessary powers to be preferably two steps ahead of terrorists at all times. Terrorists need to be stopped before they hurt. And that is the reason for the proposal that we are discussing here in the Chamber today. The draft law, as the rapporteur of the Left also said, is the implementation of initiative No. 4 in the government's terrorist outlay... A strong defense against terror... from February this year. The proposal seeks to guarantee PET's ability to monitor air passengers associated with terrorist movements. The proposal thus gives PET access to the information registered by the airlines on passengers and crew members, also known as PNR data. These are information relating to journeys to and from Denmark by air, which means that it is already registered information that we are discussing here. As I have said, we Social Democrats find it a useful tool and we listen when both Danish and foreign police point out that this is a key tool in the fight against terrorists' cynical crossing borders and planning activities. Let me just make it clear in this context that the law clearly defines what this information may be used for. So it is also clear in the legislative proposal we are discussing that it must not be used for other purposes. As you know, we have set up PET supervision and it is natural that they should also be concerned with checking that this objective is respected. We Social Democrats recognise that police cooperation across the various countries is becoming an increasingly important part of the fight against terrorism, which is why it is natural to exchange information on potentially harmful extremists across countries' police forces. For us Socialists, this proposal is primarily about the security of citizens and we can fully support the fact that the legal aspect of this bill is also in order. In these words, we Socialists support the proposal.

What is crucial is once again on my part to emphasise that terrorism is cross-border in its nature. We have a particular challenge with foreign fighters, and we also know that there is a lot of travel activity in connection with some of the more extreme environments inside and outside Denmark. Therefore, PET needs access to the knowledge behind the travel patterns of the people concerned, and that is what this is all about. It is of course also important for me to stress that PET should only have access to the information that PET needs. That means you're breaking the speed limit of 80 km/h. It is, of course, subversive to respect for speed limits, it is subversive to traffic morals. That is why it is time we looked at the speed limits with fresh eyes and made them more logical and understandable. It is also for this reason that we, together with the Danish People's Party, the Conservatives and Liberals, have tabled motions for resolutions B 131 in which, among other things, we have tabled motions for resolutions B 131. also provides for the general speed limit on roads to be raised to 90 km/h. as part of a single package we call "Far with reason." We are also proposing that the speed limit on motorway roads should be raised to 100 km/h and that approved vehicles for the so-called Tempo 100 concept, namely caravans, trailers and mobile homes, should travel 100 km/h. on motorways in order to improve traffic flow for the benefit of road safety. Finally, we also propose that several motorway sections have a speed limit of 130 km/h. There are certainly a large number of motorway sections, where you have today signposted down to 110 km/h and people with great advantage could drive 130 km/h. Specifically, I could mention the motorway section from northern Aarhus towards Djursland, where you can only drive 110 km/h, although it is a very safe and manageable motorway. And that's how there are a number of places. So all in all, this is a very good proposal which we can warmly support on the part of the Left and, as I said, it is also one of the elements of the overall resolution that we are putting forward together with the Danish People's Party.

All of us who have tried to lose people we cared about in a traffic accident know how much it hurts. That is why we Social Democrats are sceptical about raising speed limits without a solid knowledge base on the effects. We do not deny that in some cases there may be grounds for raising the speed limits in selected areas or for selected vehicles, and that is also why we have raised the speed possibilities for buses and, in particular, caravans. But this merely making a general increase in speed on the roads without a solid basis of knowledge, which means that we know what the consequences will be, we will not be involved. Against this background, I can also begin by saying that we cannot support the proposal here today. The parties of the Folketing stand together on a road safety target of max. 100 people killed in traffic by the year 2020. 100 people killed in traffic every year by the year 2020. We're on our way. In recent years we have managed to reduce the number of road deaths from over 400 to over 150, but we need to reduce the number even further. ParlaMint-DK_20150521165019	There are several different proposals in one, as often happens with motions for resolutions. There is one of them, however, which I would like to begin by pointing out raises a very important issue, which has also been mentioned several times, and this is the discussion here about the compensations that are being awarded to the injured in cases where the media have violated privacy or brought false history, etc., and about the fact that those compensations do not cover the real costs that are actually incurred in conducting the case. That is to say, if you are offended in such a case, you may lose money to get right. Some may not do so at all because they do not have the funds because they do not want to risk losing money and so on. The problem has been clarified very clearly, and we also think in the Enhedslisten that it is a problem. Of course we do. In the course of last year, as has also been mentioned, we had a working party in place with a new kind of parliamentary tool, one might say that it was trying to review an area that was defined in advance, and through such an inter-political working group anchored in the Committee on Culture, Youth, Education, the Media and Sport, it was attempted to examine some of the issues and see where one could end up and what one could agree on. It was actually a very positive experience and I simply had the pleasure of having my first title as President of something parliamentary, at all, so it was also particularly interesting in that way. Well, in the cross-party working group, we also agreed that it is a problem that you do not recover your real costs even if you win the case. That means, as I said before, that there are some who might refrain from going into matters. But that was not the view of the working group and it is still not the view of the Enhedslisten that we solve the problem by raising the compensations or, as the Left now proposes, by having them tripled. If you do so, you run the risk of getting into the situation that the compensation for violations of privacy is not in proportion to other criminal proceedings and other damages. In order to spell it out, it is possible that a television host who has been offended by, for example, Getting bikini pictures in Se and Hør, to take a nearby example, gets a bigger replacement than a person who has been raped, and I don't think any of us are interested in us ending up in that situation. That is why we do not think it is the most appropriate. That is also why the working group began to discuss whether this means transferring the power of complaint from being civil to being public. In other words, it is the prosecution who takes the case on behalf of the injured person, rather than the injured person himself having to raise a civil case. That is what the Criminal Code Council is looking at now, because we found that this was the most obvious way to solve the problem. We cannot vote in favour of the proposal as early as today. Neither could we when we were done with the work, and that is partly because we have to consider that it has the consequence that if public charges are introduced, you as a person will not be able to decide in theory or in practice whether a case should be raised. In other words, in the case of a civil case, it is also possible to refrain from taking a case if, for example, we do not have a civil case. don't want to be dragged through it all one more time. That is, of course, the negative consequence of introducing public charges, and that is why we would very much like the Criminal Code to look at it before we take a final position. But that does not change the fact that it is immediately the most obvious thing to do. When it comes to another proposal here, which I would like to draw up at the end and which deals with the increased use of name and record bans, I would say that it violates a fundamental principle of openness in the administration of justice, which I think is right, very important not only for the press's ability to write stories, but generally for us to be able to control through openness what is happening in our courts. I know there have been no examples of too little use of the minutes or names ban, that is to say there are some cases where it should have been used, but it has not been. Nor do I have any information that there is a large increase or, in general, a high number of cases where you break the record or name ban, even if you might think that the penalty was low. So we see no reason to go in and look at that now.

ParlaMint-DK_20150521165934	Thank you to the rapporteur. Sir. Simon Emil Ammitzbøll, Liberal Alliance rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521165949	The Left has tabled a motion for a resolution aimed at stepping up action against media that improperly violates privacy or brings false stories. There should be no doubt that these are two major issues which I think many are concerned with at least in the Liberal Alliance and therefore it could be important to look at what can be done. It is clear that there are many fundamental considerations in what is at stake here: fortunately, we are a free society with a free press that must have a broad framework for development, and at the same time it is clear that we should not have a society in which people can just have their privacy violated without having consequences for those who do so, and a society in which, of course, it should not be commercially worth bringing false stories about other people or violating their privacy. The problems have been put forward by the Left with five proposals on how to solve them, and I am sure the Left has thought this in the context of the dilemma I have just outlined. In the Liberal Alliance, our starting point is that one does not have the right to bring false stories about others, that one does not have the right to violate other people's privacy. And that's the law, fortunately. When that happens anyway, there must be something wrong, then something needs to be changed. Our position on this is that it would be good to look at the level of penalties and it would be good to look at the level of compensation. This means that we can readily endorse the first three sticks of the motion for a resolution tabled by the Left, i.e. a threefold increase in the level of compensation for those who are subjected to improper violations of privacy or false stories, a threefold increase in the level of penalties for the same offence and a sharp increase in the level of penalties for breaches of minutes and names. We say yes to that on the part of the Liberal Alliance. But we believe that these three proposals alone will be able to change the situation and change the practice of those who, we say, are unvorous of those who today violate privacy, those who today bring the wrong stories. It will have such a great impact on them if we make the first three amendments that we do not think it is necessary to implement the whole package in the Left's proposal. That is why we would appeal to the Left to try to see whether we could have a majority, where we are merely moving forward with these three elements of the proposal to see if this would not bring about any change in reality. If we do not do so against expectation, we will have to continue the discussion on what we can do in that case. However, it is our approach to the proposal that lies here.

ParlaMint-DK_20150521172406	The debate is open. The Minister for the Environment.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521172431	This motion for a resolution, which calls for watercourses to be used primarily for drainage purposes and for waste water to be given priority over nature and environmental considerations, states that the proposers want us to go back to before the amendment of the river basin law, which, 30 years ago, stipulated that the management of our watercourses must weigh up the various interests associated with each river, and thus not only take into account individual interests in terms of ensuring the diversion of water. Let me be clear straight away: the government cannot support the proposal, as it represents a significant step backwards for nature and the environment. Watercourses are used to divert drainage water from the soil, so it is cultivatable for the farmer, but streams are also a natural part of the Danish landscape, just as the coasts, bogs and forests are. Watercourses are therefore also available for the fish, for the plants, for nature and to ensure a good balance between wet and dry in the landscape. That is why the 1983 Watercourse Act also states that, in the administration and management of our watercourses, we must weigh up the various interests associated with each river. The various interests are equal. The aim of the law is therefore not to take account of one interest rather than the other, but to try to resolve the conflicts which may arise in connection with the use and protection of watercourses. Throughout the ages, many interests have been attached to watercourses and their surroundings. It can, for example, be done. be dewatering for the purposes of crop safety, pond farming, drainage of rain and sewage, living conditions for animals and plants in and around watercourses, sailing and fishing. The balance of interests is often complicated. I recognize that. This does not mean, however, that one interest with a magic potion should consistently and always give way to the other, which the motion for a resolution calls for. Moreover, watering is not necessarily conflicting with nature and environmental considerations. In many cases it will actually be possible to ensure a sensible drainage of the fields at the same time as a good condition in the watercourse. A change to the law, so that watering-off considerations must always take precedence regardless of nature and environmental considerations, will also make it very difficult, if not impossible, to fulfil the EU's obligations, among others. in relation to the water framework directive's objective of good status in several watercourses. This will certainly be much more expensive because, in the interests of EU commitments, we will have to invest in extensive restoration projects which, in addition to ensuring complete drainage, also ensure a good condition in the watercourse. In my view, the strength of the river basin law is precisely that it strikes a balance between the many considerations and interests. In addition to the possibility of balancing various considerations, the law also provides for the possibility of local use, where rubber boots are placed on the floor, of finding the most sensible solutions that take account of farmers, nature, the risk of flooding, etc. This will no longer be the case if the resolution is implemented here. Then it is true that in recent years we have experienced very extreme rainfall events which have resulted in large floods. I fully understand and share the concern that this will happen again. We just cannot necessarily protect ourselves from floods by keeping our streams free of natural vegetation and deepening them. Other and more comprehensive measures are needed to address extreme rainfall and climate change. Therefore, the government has ensured that the municipalities make climate adjustment plans and, as part of the climate adjustment efforts, the government opened up in 2013 that the waste water companies can finance changes to the watercourses through the wastewater tariffs, so that they can provide more room for water storage if the amount of water would alternatively end up in the city and flood the city's sewers. If, however, there is a need to intensify maintenance, so that more water runs away, then, as I have said, the law on watercourses already has the possibility of doing so. However, this must always be done after a concrete balance has been struck between local conditions. I would also like to remind you that both municipalities and private individuals can take the initiative in carrying out a regulatory procedure, for example. to expand the watercourse. As you know, it is the municipalities which, as a river basin authority, are responsible for managing the river basin law and assessing the individual case, and all municipalities must now also draw up a climate adjustment plan so that the balance can be made on a more solid and comprehensive basis. If it is the assessment that the municipalities need to be refreshed, which responsibility they have as a watercourse authority, then I would like to make sure that, for example, this is done. a letter is sent to the municipalities, reminding them of the rules and of their obligations and opportunities under the Watercourse Act. Although I think that the river basin law is working well, that does not mean, of course, that it cannot be relevant to look at whether there is a need to adjust the law on certain points. Therefore, a wide-ranging working group has also been set up under the so-called Watercourse Forum, where one of the tasks is precisely to assess whether there is a need to amend the Watercourse Act. I believe that we should wait until we know the recommendations of the working group to adopt a position on any amendment to the law. The proposers do not just want the law on water to be changed. They also want to reverse the burden of proof so that landowners do not have to prove that watercourse maintenance does not harm certain animal species in Natura 2000 areas, but instead it is up to the municipalities to prove that a change in maintenance is not contrary to EU law. But there must be a misunderstanding. It has always been the responsibility of the municipality to provide the necessary basis for establishing that the change of a watercourse maintenance will not harm the protected nature of the EU. So, if you think the protection rules are too restrictive, I would just like to remind you that the European Court of Justice has stated that there must be certainty that, for example, there must be security. a change in watercourse maintenance will not harm the protected nature of the Union. Finally, in addition to the proposals for watercourses to be used primarily for drainage purposes and for drainage purposes to be given priority over nature and environmental considerations, the proposers also want to take the watercourses out of Section 3 of the Nature Conservation Act and instead introduce a similar provision into the Watercourse Act, but with a relaxation compared to the current administration of Section 3, so drainage always takes precedence over nature protection considerations. But you need a derogation to be able to make a major change or regulation of a watercourse. If we take watercourses out of the Nature Conservation Act, we will reduce the protection of a number of streams and the animal and plant life that unfolds there. With the content of the proposal, however, blue blocs are once again purely flagging environmental policy. If the resolution is adopted here, we will roll back the protection of nature and the environment decades, both in relation to the law on watercourses and in relation to the law on nature conservation. In my opinion, the laws in force strike the right balance between the various considerations relating to the use and protection of watercourses. And on that basis, as I said, the government cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150521174230	Thank you, Minister. There are no more short remarks right now. The next speaker is Mrs Mette Reissmann, the Socialists.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521174259	Thank you for the floor. I must point out that I am also speaking on behalf of the Radical Left. Today we are debating motion for a resolution No. B 145, tabled by the Left, the Conservatives, the Danish People's Party and the Liberal Alliance. It is about how we should manage the Danish streams and how the balance should be between nature and farmers and property owners. The floods benefit the land of the landowners because they water down the land surrounding them and thus prevent floods. It has been used particularly in agriculture, where floods can obviously have major economic consequences. Throughout history, therefore, many natural streams have been accomplished, deepened, and then adapted to the needs of farmers. For like to look back a long way in history, it is special after Denmark lost Schleswig-Holstein and Lauenburg. Then a large part of the Jutland heaths were cultivated by means of e.g.: watercourses to compensate for the lost agricultural area. This has meant that two thirds of Denmark's land is agricultural land, and this is part of the reason why agriculture exported over 82 billion in 2013. kr. We therefore have a strong agricultural sector in Denmark, and we must, of course, safeguard it. However, the regulation of our watercourses has also had major consequences for the animal and plant life that belongs in the watercourses. Since 1983, the management of watercourses has therefore been required to take account of nature and environmental considerations when they are to be maintained. The waterways are protected by section 3 of the Nature Conservation Act, which means that there can be no change without a derogation from the municipality. But the parties of the right want the watercourses no longer to be included in Section 3 of the Nature Conservation Act. They are obviously not prepared for us to protect our fragile nature, and, on the contrary, if it is for the bourgeois parties, it must be the case that the watercourses must be regulated if there are economic or professional conditions that advocate change, even at the expense of nature. They want it to be made clear in the legislation that it is nature that must stand up to when watercourses are to be managed. Watercourses must be for farmers, even if they are an important part of the Danish landscape and are home to a large number of different flora and fauna. The Socialists are of the opinion that we can guarantee a strong agricultural sector even if we look after our watercourses. There are other instruments to be used. For example: We set aside 4 billion in April. kr. to the Danish agriculture until 2018, which among other things. support environmentally friendly agricultural technology and investment in modern barns. We would like to support farmers in producing high-quality food both for the benefit of Danish stomachs and foreign export markets, but not if they compromise on environmental requirements. Relaxing the nature protection of our watercourses will be a setback for the work that has been done over decades, and this can have dramatic consequences for an ecosystem that is already fragile and that has only just begun to recover after hundreds of years of default. This does not mean that we should take the concerns about the risk of flooding agricultural areas less seriously, because we know that climate change risks more severe and more frequent rainfalls, and that is why we have also, for several years, started initiatives which are precisely researching climate adjustment, even when it comes to preventing the flooding of our agricultural land. The initiatives are based on research and development, technology and intelligent solutions, and this is in stark contrast to this resolution, which is based on an outdated approach to human-natural relations. In the motion for a resolution, the bourgeois parties want the watercourses to serve primarily to water down the surrounding land. However, the watercourses should not serve more economic interests than those of nature. So when we discuss streams, there are many interests at stake that are represented by a wide range of interested organisations. Of course, all these interests must be taken into account and weighed against each other, but the interest of one group must not be taken into account per year. The definition is more important than anyone else's, and we must also in future have the balance between nature and society that we have been working on since 1983. Therefore, the Social Democrats are also of the opinion that the watercourses should continue to be part of section 3 of the Nature Conservation Act, which of course allows regular maintenance, but which requires exemption for major changes. With the proposal, the bourgeois parties show that they do not want to prioritise a rich and varied nature in the Danish landscape, and that they rather believe that our animals and plants must now suffer from economic profit. The Socialists do not agree with that position and we cannot therefore support the proposal.

It's correctly reported. Then the proposers draw the consequence of the one-sided focus on getting the watering through, but if you now read the report from the Nature and Agriculture Commission, you will see that what they are saying is that there is actually a greater need to have more focus on nature and the environment and have more focus on climate adaptation, and that is what should be reflected in new legislation in this area. I therefore agree with the authors of the motion if what they think is that we should make a change to the legislation based on the recommendations of the Nature and Agriculture Commission. Then it would be a really good idea. But why is it that exactly the opposite is proposed? I am not sure that I would have discovered it if it were not because the authors themselves have highlighted the Nature and Agriculture Commission as some of the things they think it is essential to start with. So just at the end of the day: I do not really understand why the right does not just propose that section 3 of the legislation be repealed, because all the protection of what is on land must be removed for the sake of agricultural production, and here all protection must be removed, so that the water can run away quickly, and then it does not make much sense to have any land that is to some extent covered by nature protection. But this certainly serves the good purpose of pointing out to all of us that when the parties of the Right, in a short episode of natural pleasure, have found some forest to embark on in order to create greater biodiversity, they really pay dearly when it comes to reducing biodiversity in the rest of nature. I think that is just a pity, but unfortunately it is not possible to understand the motion for a resolution very much differently from the fact that that is what it says, although I can hear the speeches that are today that you are not so proud of what you yourself have proposed. You might have suggested something else, but then you should have done it.

I will conclude from the debate here, even though it has sometimes been a rather harsh debate, and it is good enough that opinions are broken, that I think there is a way forward where we can find some wise solutions together, so that we get the water out to the sea while the environment is well.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184447	There is one more brief comment, Mr President. Per Clausen, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184451	Perhaps I find it difficult to understand the need to amend the law when we find that in Aalborg, which is a socialist-led municipality, where it is an Enhedslistenmand, who is the councilman for the area, within the given legislation, a solution has been found, as Mr. Kristian Pihl Lorentzen thinks is good. Then it is hard to imagine a new piece of legislation. Is it because there are all these Left Mayors and Left Committee chairmen around the country who can't find out that we have to change the legislation, that is because they can't really read what it says, that you have to take into account both the diversion of water and nature and the environment? I'd like to know.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184525	Thank you. The rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184526	As I have said, I have praised the municipality of Aalborg many times here, it is honored, whether you are from the Enhedslisten or what you are. But it could also be that it was because there are some sensible Leftmen in the municipal council and in the committee up there, that cannot be ruled out. But not all municipalities have the same focus on what Aalborg has. Many municipalities focus on this, and then there are some who lag behind. I think that by making the law clear, without, if I may say so, going into the second ditch, we will ensure that we have a fairly uniform and good administration of this throughout Denmark, and that is what we need. But the municipality of Aalborg is a premier municipality in this area, which I agree with, and we should like to see it spread out. We can then do this slowly and slowly, but we could also do it by saying that the good experiences we have from Aalborg we put into legislation together with other good ideas.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184619	The questioner on the second brief remark.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184621	Now the model in Aalborg has been developed precisely because of the current legislation, where both must be taken into account. Well, then, sir. Kristian Pihl Lorentzen said several times that the quality of the water in Gudenåen has become much better than when he and I were young. Kristian Pihl Lorentzen saw that the legislation should be just as it was 30 years ago? And I know that sir... Kristian Pihl Lorentzen will be sad when we say this by 30 years, but could he not explain in what way the legislation will depart from that which existed 30 years ago if we adopt this resolution?
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184656	The rapporteur.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184656-2	When I talk about changing the law, it's not such dramatic changes. I just want to make it a little clearer that we need to get that water out of here. It is not a question of rolling things back 30 years. Because we all agree that good consideration must be given to the environment. So I think that unites all of us.
ParlaMint-DK_20150521184713	Thank you to the rapporteur. As no one else has asked to speak, the debate is closed. I propose that the proposal be referred to the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Policy. Are there any comments? It's agreed.
